Destination Calculus: A Linear λ − \lambda- Calculus for Purely Functional Memory Writes
Thomas Bagrel, Arnaud Spiwack

Abstract
. Destination passing —aka. out parameters— is taking a parameter to fill rather than returning a result from a function. Due to its apparently imperative nature, destination passing has struggled to find its way to pure functional programming. In this paper, we present a pure functional calculus with destinations at its core. Our calculus subsumes all the similar systems, and can be used to reason about their correctness or extension. In addition, our calculus can express programs that were previously not known to be expressible in a pure language. This is guaranteed by a modal type system where modes are used to manage both linearity and scopes. Type safety of our core calculus was proved formally with the Coq proof assistant.



CCS:
Theory of computation Type structures
CCS:
Software and its engineering Formal language definitions
CCS:
Software and its engineering Functional languages
CCS:
Software and its engineering Data types and structures

1.
Introduction

In destination-passing style, a function doesn’t return a value: it takes as an argument a location where the output of the function ought to be written. A function of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\to}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
would, in destination-passing style, have type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\to}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\to}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}
instead, where
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
denotes a destination for value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
. This style is common in system programming, where destinations
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
are more commonly known as “out parameters” (in C,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
would typically be a pointer of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U*}}
).

The reason why system programs rely on destinations so much is that using destinations can save calls to the memory allocator. If a function returns a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
, it has to allocate the space for a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
. But with destinations, the caller is responsible for finding space for a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
. The caller may simply ask the memory allocator for the space, in which case we’ve saved nothing; but it can also reuse the space of an existing
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
that it doesn’t need anymore, or space in an array, or even space in a region of memory that the allocator doesn’t have access to, like a memory-mapped file.

This does all sound quite imperative, but we argue that the same considerations are relevant for functional programming, albeit to a lesser extent. In fact
shaikhha_destination-passing_2017
has demonstrated that using destination passing in the intermediate language of a functional array-programming language allowed for significant optimizations. Where destinations truly shine in functional programming, however, is that they increase the expressiveness of the language; destinations as first-class values allow for meaningfully new programs to be written, as first explored in
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
.

The trouble, of course, is that destinations are imperative; we wouldn’t want to sacrifice the immutability of our linked data structures (later on abbreviated
structures
) for the sake of the more situational destinations. The goal here is to extend functional programming just enough to be able to build immutable structures by destination passing without endangering purity and memory safety. This is already what
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
introduces, using a linear type system to restrict mutation. Destinations become write-once-only references into a structure with holes. Here we follow these leads, but we refine the type system further to allow for even more programs (see
Section

3
).

There are two key elements to the expressiveness of destination passing:

•

structures can be built in any order. Not only from the leaves to the root, like in ordinary functional programming, but also from the root to the leaves, or any combination thereof. This can be done in ordinary functional programming using function composition in a form of continuation-passing; and destinations act as an optimization. This line of work was pioneered by
minamide_functional_1998
. While this only increases expressiveness when combined with the next point, the optimization is significant enough that destination passing has been implemented in the Ocaml optimizer to support tail modulo constructor
(
bour_tmc_2021
)
;

•

when destinations are first-class values, they can be passed and stored like ordinary values. This is the innovation of
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
upon which we build. The consequence is that not only the order in which a structure is built is arbitrary, this order can be determined dynamically during the runtime of the program.

To support this programming style, we introduce
\lambda_{d}
. We intend
\lambda_{d}
to serve as a foundational, theoretical calculus to reason about safe destinations in a functional setting. Indeed
\lambda_{d}
subsumes all the systems that we’ve discussed in this section. As such we expect that these systems or their extensions can be justified simply by giving them a translation into
\lambda_{d}
, in order to get all the safety results and metatheory of
\lambda_{d}
for free. Even though
\lambda_{d}
is not really meant to be implemented as a real programming language, we still draft an implementation strategy for it based on efficient mutations, in the line of
(
bour_tmc_2021
;
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
.

Our contributions are as follows:

•

\lambda_{d}
, a modal, linear, simply typed
\lambda
-calculus with destinations (
Sections

5
and
6
).
\lambda_{d}
is expressive enough to serve as an encoding for previous calculi with destinations (see
);

•

a demonstration that
\lambda_{d}
is more expressive than previous calculi with destinations (
Sections

3
and
4
), namely that destinations can be stored in structures with holes. We show how we can improve, in particular, on the breadth-first traversal example of
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
;

•

an implementation strategy for
\lambda_{d}
which uses mutation without compromising the purity of
\lambda_{d}
(
);

•

formally-verified proofs, with the Coq proof assistant, of type safety (
).

2.
Working with Destinations

Let’s introduce and get familiar with
\lambda_{d}
, our simply typed
\lambda
-calculus with destination. The syntax is standard, except that we use linear logic’s
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
for sums and products, and linear function arrow
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}
, since
\lambda_{d}
is linearly typed, even though it isn’t a focus in this section.

2.1.
Building up a Vocabulary

In its simplest form, destination passing, much like continuation passing, is using a location, received as an argument, to return a value. Instead of a linear function with signature
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
, in
\lambda_{d}
you would have
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
is read “destination for type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
”.

For instance, here is a destination-passing version of the identity function:

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral dId\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral dId\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\end{array}\\

We think of a destination as a reference to an uninitialized memory location, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}
(read “fill
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}
”) as writing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}
to the memory location.

The form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}
is the simplest way to use a destination. But we don’t have to fill a destination with a complete value in a single step. Destinations can be filled piecemeal.

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithInl\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithInl\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\end{array}\\

In this example, we’re filling a destination for type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
by setting the outermost constructor to left variant
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}
. We think of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}
(read “fill
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}
”) as allocating memory to store a block of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt\phantom{l}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
, write the address of that block to the location that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
points to, and return a new destination of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
pointing to the uninitialized argument of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}
. Uninitialized memory, when part of a structure or value, like
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt\phantom{l}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt\phantom{l}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
, is called a
hole
.

Notice that with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithInl\pdfliteral}}
we are constructing the structure from the outermost constructor inward: we’ve written a value of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt\phantom{l}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
into a hole, but we have yet to describe what goes in the new hole
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt\phantom{l}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
. Such data constructors with uninitialized arguments are called
hollow constructors
1
1

1

The full triangle
\blacktriangleleft
is used to fill a destination with a fully-formed value, while the
hollow
triangle
\triangleleft
is used to fill a destination with a
hollow constructor
.
. This is opposite to how functional programming usually works, where values are built from the innermost constructors outward: first we make a value
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}
and only then can we use
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}
to make
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}
. This will turn out to be a key ingredient in the expressiveness of destination passing.

Yet, everything we’ve shown so far could have been done with continuations. So it’s worth asking: how are destinations different from continuations? Part of the answer lies in our intention to effectively implement destinations as pointers to uninitialized memory (see
). But where destinations really differ from continuations is when one has several destinations at hand. Then they have to fill
all
the destinations; whereas when one has multiple continuations, they can only return to one of them. Multiple destination arises when a destination for a pair gets filled with a hollow pair constructor:

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{({,})}}\end{array}\\

After using
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair\pdfliteral}}
, both the first field
and
the second field must be filled, using the destinations of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
respectively. The key remark here is that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair\pdfliteral}}
couldn’t exist if we replaced destinations by continuations, as we couldn’t use both returned continuations easily.

Structures with Holes

It is crucial to note that while a destination is used to build a structure, the type of the structure being built might be different from the type of the destination that is being filled. A destination of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
is a pointer to a yet-undefined part of a bigger structure. We say that such a structure has a hole of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
; but the type of the structure itself isn’t specified (and never appears in the signature of destination-filling functions). For instance, using
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair\pdfliteral}}
only indicates that the structure being operated on has a hole of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
that is being written to.

Thus, we still need a type to tie the structure under construction — left implicit by destination-filling primitives — with the destinations representing its holes. To represent this,
\lambda_{d}
introduces a type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
for a structure of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
missing a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
to be complete. There can be several holes in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
— resulting in several destinations on the right hand side — and as long as there remains holes in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
, it cannot be read. For instance,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}
represents a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
that misses both a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
and a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
to be complete (thus to be readable).

The general form
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
is read “
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
ampar
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
”. The name “ampar” stands for “asymmetric memory par”; we will explain how we came up with this type and name in
. A similar connective is called
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Incomplete}}
in
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
. For now, it’s sufficient to observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
is akin to a “par” type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{\&}T^{\perp}}}
in linear logic; you can think of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
as a (linear) function from
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
. That structures with holes could be seen as linear functions was first observed in
(
minamide_functional_1998
)
; we elaborate on the value of having a “par” type with access to first-class destinations, rather than just linear functions to represent structures with holes, in
Section

4
.

Destinations always exist within the context of a structure with holes. A destination is both a witness of a hole present in the structure, and a handle to write to it. Crucially, destinations are otherwise ordinary values. To access the destinations of an ampar,
\lambda_{d}
provides a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
construction, which lets us apply a function to the right-hand side of the ampar. It is in the body of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
that functions operating on destinations can be called to update the structure:

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithInl^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithInl^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithInl\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\end{array}\\
\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral fillWithPair\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\end{array}\\

To tie this up, we need a way to introduce and to eliminate structures with holes. Structures with holes are introduced with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
which creates a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
is a bit like the identity function: it is a hole (of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
) that needs a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
to be a complete value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
. Memory-wise, it is an uninitialized block large enough to host a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
, and a destination pointing to it. Conversely, structures with holes are eliminated with
2
2

2

As the name suggest, there is a more general elimination
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
. It will be discussed in
Section

5
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
: if all the destinations have been consumed and only unit remains on the right side, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}
no longer has holes and thus is just a normal, complete structure.

Equipped with these, we can, for instance, derive traditional constructors from piecemeal filling. In fact,
\lambda_{d}
doesn’t have primitive constructor forms, constructors in
\lambda_{d}
are syntactic sugar. We show here the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{({,})}}
, but the other constructors are derived similarly. Operator
\,\fatsemi\,
, present in second example, is used to chain operations returning unit type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}
.

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}})\end{array}\\
\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{({,})}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{({,})}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{y}})\end{array}\end{array}\\

Memory Safety and Purity

At this point, the reader may be forgiven for feeling distressed at all the talk of mutations and uninitialized memory. How is it consistent with our claim to be building a pure and memory-safe language? The answer is that it wouldn’t be if we’d allow unrestricted use of destinations. Instead
\lambda_{d}
uses a linear type system to ensure that:

•

destinations are written at least once, preventing examples like:

\hskip-17.7005pt\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral forget\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral forget\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}})\end{array}\\

where reading the result of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral forget\pdfliteral}}
would result in reading the location pointed to by a destination that we never used, in other words, reading uninitialized memory;

•

destinations are written at most once, preventing examples like:

\hskip-17.7005pt\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral ambiguous1\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral ambiguous1\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}})\end{array}\\
\hskip-17.7005pt\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral ambiguous2\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral ambiguous2\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral let\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral in\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}})\end{array}\\

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral ambiguous1\pdfliteral}}
would return
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral ambiguous2\pdfliteral}}
would return
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}
due to evaluation order, even though let-expansion should be valid in a pure language.

2.2.
Tail-Recursive Map

Now that we have an intuition of how destinations work, let’s see how they can be used to build usual data structures. For this section, we suppose that
\lambda_{d}
has equirecursive types and a fixed-point operator. These aren’t part of the formal system of
Section

5
but don’t add any complication.

Linked Lists

We define lists as a fixpoint, as usual:
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\boldsymbol{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}
. For convenience, we also define filling operators
\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}
and
\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}}
:

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\end{array}\\

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inr}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{({,})}}\end{array}\\

Just like we did in
Section

2.1
we can recover traditional constructors from filling operators, e.g.:

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dx}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dxs}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dx}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dxs}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}})\end{array}\end{array}\\

A Tail-Recursive Map Function

List being ubiquitous in functional programming, the fact that the most natural way to write a map function on lists isn’t tail recursive (hence consumes unbounded stack space), is unpleasant. Map can be made tail-recursive in two passes: first build the result list in reverse, then reverse it. But destinations let us avoid this two-pass process altogether, as they let us extend the tail of the result list directly.
We give a complete implementation in
Figure

1
.

The main function is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}
, it has type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.45206pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}
. That is, instead of returning a resulting list, it takes a destination as an input and fills it with the result. At each recursive call,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}
creates a new hollow cons cell to fill the destination. A destination pointing to the tail of the new cons cell is also created, on which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}
is called (tail) recursively. This is really the same algorithm that you could write to implement map on a mutable list in an imperative language. Nevertheless
\lambda_{d}
is a pure language with only immutable types.

To obtain the regular
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map\pdfliteral}}
function, all is left to do is to call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
to create an initial destination, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}
, much like when we make constructors out of filling operators, like
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}}
above.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\\
\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{l}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dl}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{l}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dl}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}\,,\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dl}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dx}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dxs}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dx}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dxs}}\}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{l}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dl}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral map^{\prime}\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{l}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dl}})\end{array}\\

Figure 1
.
Tail-recursive map function on lists

2.3.
Functional Queues, with Destinations

Implementations for a tail-recursive map are present in most previous work, from
(
minamide_functional_1998
)
, to recent work
(
bour_tmc_2021
;
leijen_trmc_2023
;
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
. Tail-recursive map doesn’t need the full power of
\lambda_{d}
’s first-class destinations: it just needs a notion of structures with a (single) hole. In
Section

4
, we will build an example which fully uses first-class destinations, but first, we will need some more material.

Difference Lists

Just like in any language, iterated concatenation of lists

(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathop{+\!+}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs_{{\mathrm{2}}}}})\mathop{+\!+}\ldots)\mathop{+\!+}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}}_{n}

is quadratic in
\lambda_{d}
. The usual solution to this is difference lists. The name difference lists covers many related implementations, but in pure functional languages, a difference list is usually represented as a function
(
hughes_dlist_1986
)
. A singleton difference list is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}}}\,_{\,\,}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}}
, and concatenation of difference lists is function composition. A difference list is turned into a list by applying it to the empty list. The consequence is that no matter how many compositions we have, each cons cell will be allocated a single time, making the iterated concatenation linear indeed.

However, each concatenation allocates a closure. If we’re building a difference list from singletons and composition, there’s roughly one composition per cons cell, so iterated composition effectively performs two traversals of the list. In
\lambda_{d}
, we can do better by representing a difference list as a list with a hole. A singleton difference list is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt\phantom{l}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
. Concatenation is filling the hole with another difference list, using operator
\mathop{\triangleleft\raisebox{0.39835pt}{$\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\circ}$}}
. The details are on the left of
Figure

2
. The
\lambda_{d}
encoding for difference lists makes no superfluous traversal: concatenation is just an
O(1)
in-place update.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\\
\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral append\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral append\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{y}}~\boldsymbol{\triangleq}\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dys}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dys}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(::)}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dy}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dys^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dy}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{y}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dys^{\prime}}}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral concat\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\\

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral toList\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{List}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\\

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral dequeue\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Queue}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Queue}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\\
