\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{q}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inr}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\,,\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral toList\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{ys}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\,,\\
\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inr}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{xs}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{DList}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\}\}\end{array}\end{array}\\

Figure 2
.
Difference list and queue implementation in equirecursive
\lambda_{d}

Efficient Queue Using Difference Lists

In an immutable functional language, a queue can be implemented as a pair of lists
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{front}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{back}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}

(
hood_queue_1981
)
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{back}}
stores new elements in reverse order (
O(1)
prepend). We pop elements from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{front}}
, except when it is empty, in which case we set the queue to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral reverse\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{back}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}
, and pop from the new front.

For their simple implementation, Hood-Melville queues are surprisingly efficient: the cost of the reverse operation is
O(1)
amortized for a single-threaded use of the queue. Still, it would be better to get rid of this full traversal of the back list. Taking a step back, this
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{back}}
list that has to be reversed before it is accessed is really merely a representation of a list that can be extended from the back. And we already know an efficient implementation for this: difference lists.

So we can give an improved version of the simple functional queue using destinations. This implementation is presented on the right-hand side of
Figure

2
. Note that contrary to an imperative programming language, we can’t implement the queue as a single difference list: as mentioned earlier, our type system prevents us from reading the front elements of difference lists. Just like for the simple functional queue, we need a pair of one list that we can read from, and one that we can extend. Nevertheless this implementation of queues is both pure, as guaranteed by the
\lambda_{d}
type system, and nearly as efficient as what an imperative programming language would afford.

3.
Scope Escape of Destinations

In
Section

2
, we’ve been making an implicit assumption: establishing a linear discipline on destinations ensures that all destinations will eventually find their way to the left of a fill operator
\blacktriangleleft
or
\triangleleft
, so that the associated holes get written to. This turns out to be slightly incomplete.

To see why, let’s consider the type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
: the type of a destination pointing to a hole where a destination is expected. Think of it as an equivalent of the pointer type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T*\!*}}
in the C language. Destinations are indeed ordinary values, so they can be stored in data structures, and before they get stored, holes stand in their place in the structure. For instance, if we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
, we can form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
will be stored in the structure pointed to by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}
.

Should we count
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
as linearly used here? The alternatives don’t seem promising:

•

If we count this as a non-linear use of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
is rejected since destinations (represented here by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
) can only be used linearly. This choice is fairly limiting, as it would prevent us from storing destinations in structures with holes, as we do, crucially, in
Section

4
. Nonetheless, that’s the option chosen in
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
.

•

If we do not count this use of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
at all, we can write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}
so that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
is both stored for later use
and
filled immediately (resulting in the corresponding hole being potentially written to twice), which is unsound, as discussed in
Section

2.1
.

So linear use it is. But it creates a problem: there’s no way, within our linear type system, to distinguish between “a destination has been used on the left of a triangle so its corresponding hole has been filled” and “a destination has been stored and its hole still exists at the moment”. This oversight may allow us to read uninitialized memory!

Let’s compare two examples. We assume a simple store semantics for now where structures with holes stay in the store until they are completed. We’ll need the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
operator. The semantics of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}
is: allocate a structure with a single root hole in the store, call the supplied function with the destination to the root hole as an argument; when the function has consumed all destinations (so only unit remains), pop the structure from the store to obtain a complete
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
.

In this snippet, structures with holes are given names
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}
in the store; holes are given names too and denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
, and concrete destinations are denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}
.

When the building scope of
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}
is parent to the one of
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
, everything works well because
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}
, that contains destination pointing to
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
, has to be consumed before
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}
can be read:

\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{crcl}&$\lx@text@lbrace \lx@text@rbrace$&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}}\,_{\,\,}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}}\,_{\,\,}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}})\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}})\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\}&|&({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}}\,_{\,\,}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}})\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}})\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}},{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\}&|&({\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}})\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}},{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\}&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\}&|&{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\}&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\\
\to&$\lx@text@lbrace \lx@text@rbrace$&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\end{array}

However, when
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}
’s scope is parent to
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}
’s, we can write a linearly typed yet unsound program, as we demonstrate in the following example.

\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{crcl}&$\lx@text@lbrace \lx@text@rbrace$&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}}\,_{\,\,}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}}\,_{\,\,}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}})\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\,,~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\})\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\}&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral alloc\pdfliteral}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}}\,_{\,\,}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}})\boldsymbol{:}{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Bool}}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\,,~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}},{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\}&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{hd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\,,~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}},{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\}&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\,,~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\\
\to&\{{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\}&|&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\,,~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral deref\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{vd}}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\raisebox{-3.09999pt}{\scalebox{0.35}{\includegraphics[width=17.0pt]{bc-bombe}\includegraphics[width=17.0pt]{bc-bombe}\includegraphics[width=17.0pt]{bc-bombe}}}\end{array}

Here the expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dd}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
results in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}
escaping its scope for the parent one, so
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}
is just uninitialized memory (the hole
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.29167pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.29167pt}}}
) when we dereference it. This example must be rejected by our type system.

Again, using purely a linear type system, we can only reject this example if we also reject the first, sound example, as in
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
. In this case, the type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
becomes practically useless: such destinations can never be filled.

This isn’t the direction we want to take: we really want to be able to store destinations in data structures with holes. So we want
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}
to be allowed to be linear. Without further restrictions, it wouldn’t be sound, so to address this,
\lambda_{d}
uses a system of ages to represent scopes. Ages are described in
Section

5
.

4.
Breadth-First Tree Traversal

As a full-fledged example, which uses the full expressive power of
\lambda_{d}
, we borrow and improve on an example from
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
, breadth-first tree relabeling:

“
Given a tree, create a new one of the same shape, but with the values at the nodes replaced by the numbers
1\ldots|T|
in breadth-first order.
”

This isn’t a very natural problem in functional programming, as breadth-first traversal implies that the order in which the structure must be built (left-to-right, top-to-bottom) is not the same as the structural order of a functional tree — building the leaves first and going up to the root. So it usually requires fancy functional workarounds
(
okasaki_bfs_2000
;
jones_gibbons_linearbfs_93
;
gibbons_phases_2023
)
.

It’s very tempting to implement this example in an efficient imperative-like fashion, where a queue drives the processing order, thanks to the power of destinations. For that,
minamide_functional_1998
’s system where structures with holes are represented as linear functions is not enough. Destinations as first-class values are very much required.

Figure

3
presents the
\lambda_{d}
implementation of the breadth-first tree traversal. The core idea is that we hold a queue of pairs, storing each an input subtree with (a destination to) its corresponding output subtree. When the element
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tree}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dtree}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}
at the front of the queue has been processed, the children nodes of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tree}}
and children destinations of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dtree}}
are enqueued to be processed later. There,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
is defined unsurprisingly as
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\boldsymbol{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}
; we refer to the constructors of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Nil}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Node}}
, defined in the obvious way. We also assume some encoding of the type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Nat}}
of natural number.
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Queue}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
is the efficient queue type from
Section

2.3
.

We implement the actual breadth-first relabeling
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral relabelDPS\pdfliteral}}
as an instance of a more general breadth-first traversal function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral mapAccumBFS\pdfliteral}}
, which applies any state-passing style transformation of labels in breadth-first order.

In
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral mapAccumBFS\pdfliteral}}
, we create a new destination
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dtree}}
into which we will write the result of the traversal, then call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral go\pdfliteral}}
. The
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral go\pdfliteral}}
function is in destination-passing style, but what’s remarkable is that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral go\pdfliteral}}
takes an unbounded number of destinations as arguments, since there are as many destinations as items in the queue. This is where we use the fact that destinations are ordinary values.

The implementation of
Figure

3
is very close to the one found in
(
bagrel_destination-passing_2024
)
. The difference is that, because they can’t store destinations in structures with holes (see the discussion in
Section

3
), their implementation can’t use the efficient queue implementation from
Section

2.3
. So they have to revert to using a Hood-Melville queue for breadth-first traversal.

However this improvement comes at a cost: we a introduce
mode
system that combines linearity and age to make the system sound, hence the new
fuchsia
annotations in the code. We’ll describe modes in detail in
Section

5
. In the meantime,
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}
control linearity: we use
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}
to mean that the state and function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}
can be used many times. On the other hand,
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}
is an
age
annotation; in particular, the associated argument cannot carry destinations. Arguments with no modes are otherwise linear and can capture destinations. We introduce the exponential modality
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
to reify mode
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}
in a type; this is useful to return several values having different modes from a function, like in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}
. An exponential is rarely needed in an argument position, as we have
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}
.

\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral go\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Queue}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral go\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{st}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{q}}~\mathrel{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\triangleq}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{rec}$}\vss}}}~\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral dequeue\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{q}})~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~\{\\

\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}\phantom{\texttt{aaa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral go\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{st}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral singleton\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tree}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{dtree}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}))\end{array}\end{array}\\
\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral relabelDPS\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Tree}}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Nat}}\\

Figure 3
.
Breadth-first tree traversal in destination-passing style

5.
Type System

Core grammar of terms:

\!\begin{array}[]{rrl}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{u}}&\mathrel{\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\hskip 6.97498pt|\hskip 6.97498pt{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\hskip 6.97498pt|\hskip 6.97498pt{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\,\fatsemi\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}\\

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}j}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}j}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p}}\hskip-0.58122pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.40002pt\cdot\hskip-0.40002pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.58122pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}\boldsymbol{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.40002pt\cdot\hskip-0.40002pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}\hskip-0.58122pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.40002pt\cdot\hskip-0.40002pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p}}\hskip-0.59998pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.59998pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}}}\boldsymbol{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{p^{\prime}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}\hskip-0.58122pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{a^{\prime}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}

.

Operations on typing contexts:

{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{n}}

\cdot

\mathord{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}

\boldsymbol{\triangleq}

\boldsymbol{\triangleq}

.

Figure 4
.
Terms, types and modes of
\lambda_{d}

\lambda_{d}
is a simply typed
\lambda
-calculus with unit (
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}
), product (
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}
) and sum (
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}
) types. Its most salient features are the destination
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}
and ampar
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
types which we’ve introduced in
Sections

2
,
3
and
4
.

To ensure that destinations are used soundly, we need both to enforce the linearity of destination but also to prevent destinations from escaping their scope, as discussed in
Section

3
. To that effect,
\lambda_{d}
tracks the
age
of destinations, that is how many nested scope have been open between the current expression and the scope from which a destination originates. We’ll see in
Section

5.2
that scopes are introduced by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}
. For instance, if we have a term
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}
, then we will say that the innermost occurrence of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}
has age
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}2}}
because two nested
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
separate the definition and use site of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}
.

A natural idea, to track ages, is to introduce a modality
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\uparrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
to mean “a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
in the previous scope”. Let’s explore why this isn’t quite going to work for us, hence why we need something more general.

A typical presentation of modal type theories is with a pair of context
\Gamma_{\uparrow}
;
\Gamma
of bindings from the previous scope and the current scope respectively
(
pfenning_modal_1995
)
, and rules such as

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{minipage}[103.50105pt]\begin{equation}\frac{\phantom{a}}{\Gamma_{\uparrow}~;~\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\boldsymbol{\vdash}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}}\end{equation}\end{minipage}&\begin{minipage}[103.50105pt]\begin{equation}\frac{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}~;~\Gamma_{\uparrow}\boldsymbol{\vdash}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}}{\Gamma_{\uparrow}~;~\Gamma\boldsymbol{\vdash}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\uparrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}}\end{equation}\end{minipage}\end{array}

The idea is that only variables from the current scope can be used to make a term for the current scope (
), and to make a term at the previous scope, you need to make it only with variables from
\Gamma_{\uparrow}
(
). But this can’t be the whole story here. Indeed, there’s no way to refer to variables from two scopes ago, and it would be unsound for
\lambda_{d}
, in the manner described in
Section

3
, to mash all the older scopes together in
\Gamma_{\uparrow}
. So, following this route, we’d need infinitely many contexts (and as many modalities, or more realistically a single graded modality), sequents would look like
\ldots~;~\Gamma_{2}~;~\Gamma_{1}~;~\Gamma_{0}\boldsymbol{\vdash}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
. Finicky, but manageable perhaps. But it’s not all! We need yet another context: one for bindings which are ageless because they don’t contain destinations, so that we can fill destinations with harmless values like
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}
that have been bound two or more scopes ago. More even: linear logic, famously, is also a modal logic (the modality is the exponential
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
), so we’d need to double each of these contexts. This would be quite messy.

Fortunately, there’s a way to simplify all this. First observe that having several contexts is the same as having a single context but with annotation on the bindings: instead of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}~;~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{y}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}~;~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{z}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}\boldsymbol{\vdash}\ldots
, we can have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}:_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{y}}:_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{z}}:_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}\boldsymbol{\vdash}\ldots
without adding or losing any information (note how the semicolons separating contexts are replaced by commas separating bindings). We’ll call these annotations
modes
. We build on the key insight, which seem to originate with
(
ghica_bounded_2014
)
, that equipping the set of modes with a particular algebraic structure is sufficient to express, algebraically, all the context manipulation that we need. They use a rig structure; for
\lambda_{d}
we don’t need a
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{0}}
element, but we’ll need a partial order on modes. The motivation for this structure in
(
ghica_bounded_2014
)
is generating electronic circuits, the same approach has since been used, for instance, for Linear Haskell
(
bernardy_linear_2018
)
where it is used to support mode polymorphism. In both of those cases, this algebraic mode style is used in the context of linear types, but
bernardy_modality_2020
show that it can generalize to a variety of modalities.

For
\lambda_{d}
, we’re following this approach:
\lambda_{d}
has a single modality
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}
indexed by a mode. This will greatly simplify our context management (especially in the computer-mechanized proofs), but, just as interestingly, we can define the set of modes as being the Cartesian product of the set of multiplicities (which keep track of linearity) and of the set of ages. The algebraic structure carries over by a generic theorem on products. This means that we can define multiplicities and ages independently, and the combination is taken care of for free.
The syntax of
\lambda_{d}
terms is presented in
Figure

4
, including the syntactic sugar that we’ve been using in
Sections

2
,
3
and
4
.

5.1.
Modes and the Age Semiring

The precise algebraic structure that we’ll be needing on modes is both a commutative additive semigroup
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{+}}
and a multiplicative monoid
({\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.44446pt\cdot\hskip-0.44446pt}}}}\,,{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}})
, with the usual distributivity law
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.44446pt\cdot\hskip-0.44446pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{)}}={\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.44446pt\cdot\hskip-0.44446pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.44446pt\cdot\hskip-0.44446pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}
. In addition we require a partial order
⥶
, such that
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{+}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.44446pt\cdot\hskip-0.44446pt}}}}
are order-preserving. In other words, we’ll be dealing with ordered semirings
3
3

3

This literature is complicated by disputed terminology, where some prefer to use the term “semiring” when the additive semigroup has a zero. This terminology is arguably more popular, but leaves no term for the version without a zero. We’ll follow the convention, in this article, that semirings with a zero are called “rigs”.
. In the rest of the article, we’ll just say “semiring”. In practice, all our semirings will be commutative, and we won’t be paying attention to the order of factors in mode multiplication.

Our mode semiring is, as promised, the product of a multiplicity semiring, to track linearity, and an age semiring, to prevent scope escape. The multiplicity semiring has elements
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}
(linear) and
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}
(unrestricted), it’s the same semiring as in
(
qtt_2018
)
or
(
bernardy_linear_2018
)
. It’s mostly unsurprising, the key is that that
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}
will enforce that a linear variable can only be used once, the full description of the multiplicity semiring is given in
Figure

4
.

Ages are more interesting. We write ages as
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}}
(with
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}
a natural number), for “defined
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}
scopes ago”.
We also have an age
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}
for variables that don’t originate from a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}
i.e. that aren’t destinations, and can be freely used in and returned by any scope. The main role of age
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}
is thus to act as a guarantee that a value doesn’t contain destinations. Finally, we will write
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}\boldsymbol{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}0}}
(“now”) for the age of destinations that originate from the current scope; and
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}\boldsymbol{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}1}}
.

The operations or order aren’t the usual ones on natural numbers though. It is crucial that
\lambda_{d}
tracks the precise age of variables. Variables from
2
scopes ago cannot be used as if they were from
1
scope ago, or vice-versa. The ordering reflects this with finite ages being arranged in a flat order, with
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}
being bigger than all of them. Multiplication of ages will reflect nesting of scope, as such, (finite) ages are multiplied by adding their numerical exponents
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\hskip-0.44446pt\cdot\hskip-0.44446pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}j}}={\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}+{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}j}}}
. In the typing rules, the most common form of scope nesting is opening a scope, which is represented by multiplying by
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}
(that is, adding
1
to the ages seen as a natural numbers). Finally
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{+}}
is used to share a variable between two subterms, it’s given by the least upper bound (for the age order above). The intuition here, is still precise age tracking: a variable must be at the same age in both subterms, or it can be
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}
, and assume whichever age it needs, including different ones in different subterms.

Bindings in the context are annotated by a mode. The insight of
(
ghica_bounded_2014
)
is that mode addition and multiplication by a mode (aka
scaling
) lift to contexts pointwise, so we have all the tools we need to define a modal type system, including a sub-structural one like linear logic.

The operations and preorders on mode, contexts, etc. are presented in
Figure

4
.
We will usually omit mode annotations when the mode is the multiplicative unit
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.6458pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}
of the semiring.

5.2.
Typing Rules

The typing rules for
\lambda_{d}
are highly inspired from
bernardy_modality_2020
and Linear Haskell
(
bernardy_linear_2018
)
, and are detailed in
Figure

5
. In particular, we use the same algebraic approach on contexts for mode tracking. Per
Section

5.1
, a mode is a pair of a multiplicity and an age.

\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{\vdash}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}

(
Typing judgment for terms

9.24994pt\Gamma_{{\mathrm{22}}}\,\boldsymbol{\vdash}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{21}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{22}}}\,\boldsymbol{\vdash}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{fraction={===},narrower=0.3,lab={{\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Ty-sterm-Prod}{{{Ty-sterm-Prod}}}}{\text{Prod}}},}

Figure 5.
Typing rules of
\lambda_{d}

Figure

5
presents the typing rules, including rules for syntactic sugar forms. We’ll now walk through the few peculiarities of the type system for terms.

Predicate
\mathtt{DisposableOnly}~\Gamma
in rules
Ty-term-Var
,
Ty-term-NewA
and
Ty-sterm-Unit
says that
\Gamma
can only contain bindings with multiplicity
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}
, for which weakening is allowed in linear logic. We only need weakening in these three rules, as they are the only possible leaves of the typing tree.

Rule
Ty-term-Var
, in addition to weakening, allows for coercion of the mode
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}
of the variable used, with ordering constraint
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.6458pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}\mathrel{\texttt{⥶}}{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}
as defined in
Figure

4
. Notably, mode coercion still doesn’t allow for a finite age to be changed to another, as
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}j}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}}
are not comparable w.r.t.
\mathrel{\texttt{⥶}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{a}}}}
when
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}j}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}
.

Rule
Ty-term-PatU
is the elimination for unit, and is also used to chain fill operations.

Pattern-matching with rules
Ty-term-App
,
Ty-term-PatS
,
Ty-term-PatP
and
Ty-term-PatE
is parametrized by a mode
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}
by which the typing context
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
of the scrutinee is multiplied. The variables which bind the subcomponents of the scrutinee then inherit this mode. In particular, this allows distributing the
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}
modality over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}
, which is not part of Girard’s intuitionistic linear logic, but is included in
(
bernardy_linear_2018
)
and referred to as
deep
modes in
(
lorenzen_oxidizing_2024
)
.

Rules for Scoping

As destinations always exist in the context of a structure with holes, and must stay in that context, we need a formal notion of
scope
. Scopes are created by
Ty-term-UpdA
, as destinations are only ever accessed through
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
. More precisely,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}
creates a new scope which spans over
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}
. In that scope,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}
has age
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}
(now), and the ages of the existing bindings in
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are multiplied by
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}
(i.e. we add
1
to ages seen as a numbers). That is represented by
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}
typing in
{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.6458pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}\hskip-1.29167pt\cdot\hskip-1.29167pt\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}:\!_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.45206pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
while the parent term
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}
types in unscaled contexts
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. This difference of age between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}
— introduced by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
, containing destinations — and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
lets us see what originates from older scopes. Specifically, distinguishing the age of destinations is crucial when typing filling primitives to avoid the pitfalls of
Section

3
.

Figure 6
.
Scope rules for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
in
\lambda_{d}

Figure

6
illustrates scopes introduced by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
, and how the typing rules for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
and
\blacktriangleleft
interact. Anticipating
Section

6.1
, ampar values are pairs with a structure with holes on the left, and destinations on the right. With
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
we enter a new scope where the destinations are accessible, but the structure with holes remains in the outer scope. As a result, when filling a destination with
Ty-term-FillLeaf
, for instance
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d_{{\mathrm{11}}}}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}
in
Figure

6
, we type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d_{{\mathrm{11}}}}}
in the new scope, while we type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}
in the outer scope, as it’s being moved to the structure with holes on the left of the ampar, which lives in the outer scope too. This is the opposite of the scaling that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
does: while
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
creates a new scope for its body, operator
\blacktriangleleft
, and similarly,
\triangleleft\raisebox{0.39835pt}{$\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\circ}$}
and
\triangleleft({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{u}})
4
4

4

We chose the form
\triangleleft({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}\!\!\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{u}})
for function creation so that any data can be built through piecemeal destination filling
, transfer their right operand to the outer scope. In other words, the right-hand side of
\blacktriangleleft
or
\triangleleft
is an enclave for the parent scope.

When using
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}
(rule
Ty-sterm-FromA’
), the left of an ampar is extracted to the current scope: this is the fundamental reason why the left of an ampar has to “take place” in the current scope. We know the structure is complete and can be extracted because the right-hand side is of type unit (
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}
), and thus no destination on the right-hand side means no hole can remain on the left.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}
is implemented in terms of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
in
Figure

4
to keep the core calculus tidier (and limit the number of typing rules, evaluation contexts, etc), but it can be implemented much more efficiently in a real-world implementation.

When an ampar is eliminated with the more general
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
in rule
Ty-term-FromA
however, we extract both sides of the ampar to the current scope, even though the right-hand side is normally in a different scope. This is only safe to do because the right-hand side is required to have type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.45206pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}
, which means it is scope-insensitive: it can’t contain any scope-controlled resource. This also ensures that the right-hand side cannot contain destinations, so the structure is ready to be read.

In
Ty-term-ToA
, on the other hand, there is no need to bother with scopes: the operator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral to\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}
embeds an already completed structure in an ampar whose left side is the structure (that continues to type in the current scope), and right-hand side is unit.

The remaining operators
\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}},\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}},\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inr}},\triangleleft\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Mod}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{m}}}\,,\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{({,})}}
from rules
Ty-term-Fill
*
are the other destination-filling primitives. They write a hollow constructor to the hole pointed by the destination operand, and return the potential new destinations that are created for new holes in the hollow constructor (or unit if there is none).

6.
Operational Semantics

Before we define the operational semantics of
\lambda_{d}
we need to introduce a few more concepts. We’ll need commands
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}{\scriptstyle\bigl[}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}{\scriptstyle\bigr]}
, they’re described in
Section

6.2
; and we’ll need runtime values (we’ll often just say
values
), described in
Section

6.1
. Indeed, the terms of
\lambda_{d}
lack any way to represent destinations or holes, or really any kind of value (for instance
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}
has been, so far, just syntactic sugar for a term
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}\ldots)
). It’s a peculiarity of
\lambda_{d}
that values (in particular, data constructors) only exist during the reduction; usually they are part of the term syntax of functional languages. We also extend the type system to cover commands and values, so as to be able to state and prove type safety theorems.

6.1.
Runtime Values and New Typing Context Forms

Grammar extended with values:
