1.01749pt\rrparenthesis~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{+}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathtt{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{,}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{+}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathtt{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{)}}\,\big]&\text{{{{\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:FillP-Red}{{{FillP-Red}}}}{}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\mathop{\triangleleft\raisebox{0.39835pt}{$\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\circ}$}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}\,\big]~~\longrightarrow~~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\hskip 1.01749pt\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.36617pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}\hskip 1.01749pt\rrparenthesis~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}\,\big]&\text{{{{\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:FillComp-Red}{{{FillComp-Red}}}}{}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\,\big]~~\longrightarrow~~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\hskip 1.01749pt\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}_{\{\,\}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\hskip 1.01749pt\rrparenthesis~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{()}}\,\big]&\text{{{{\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:FillLeaf-Red}{{{FillLeaf-Red}}}}{}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}\,{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral with\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}\,\big]~~\longrightarrow~~{\scriptstyle\bigl(}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\hskip 3.6999pt\circ\hskip 3.6999pt^{\text{op}\!}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.36617pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~\raisebox{0.69371pt}{$\scriptstyle[]$}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\scriptstyle\bigr)}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}]\,\big]&\text{{{{\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Ampar-Open}{{{Ampar-Open}}}}{}}}}\\
{\scriptstyle\bigl(}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\hskip 3.6999pt\circ\hskip 3.6999pt^{\text{op}\!}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~\raisebox{0.69371pt}{$\scriptstyle[]$}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}{\scriptstyle\bigr)}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\,\big]~~\longrightarrow~~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}~\big[\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}\,\big]&\text{{{{\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Ampar-Close}{{{Ampar-Close}}}}{}}}}\\
\end{array}$
}}\vskip-14.22636pt
$$\text{{where}}\hskip 9.24994pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral max\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral hnames\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\cup}}\,\{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{+}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathtt{1}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral max\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\cup}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral hnames\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\cup}}\,\{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{+}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathtt{1}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime\prime}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral max\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\cup}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral hnames\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{+}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathtt{1}}\end{array}\right.$$
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{9}}{Small-step semantics}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 9}}{\small Small-step semantics}}}
\end{figure}
\par Reduction rules for function application, pattern-matching, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral to\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral from\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$ are straightforward.
\par We introduce a special substitution ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\hskip 1.01749pt\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\hskip 1.01749pt\rrparenthesis$ that is used to update structures under construction, that are attached to open ampar focusing components in the stack. Such a substitution is triggered when a destination ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}$ is filled in the term under focus, typically in destination-filling primitives reductions, and results in the value ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$ being written to hole ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}$. The value ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$ may contain holes itself (e.g. when the hollow constructor ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Inl}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{+}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathtt{1}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}$ is being written to the hole ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}$ in {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillL-Red}{{{{FillL-Red}}}}}), hence the set ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}$ tracks the potential hole names introduced by value ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$, and is used to update the hole name set of the corresponding (open) ampar. Proper definition of ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\hskip 1.01749pt\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\hskip 1.01749pt\rrparenthesis$ is given in \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:sem}.
\par{\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillU-Red}{{{{FillU-Red}}}}} and {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillF-Red}{{{{FillF-Red}}}}} do not create any new hole; they only write a value to an existing one. On the other hand, rules {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillL-Red}{{{{FillL-Red}}}}}, {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillR-Red}{{{{FillR-Red}}}}}, {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillE-Red}{{{{FillE-Red}}}}} and {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillP-Red}{{{{FillP-Red}}}}} all write a hollow constructor to the hole ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}$ that contains new holes. Thus, we need to generate fresh names for these new holes, and also return a destination for each new hole with a matching name.
\par The substitution ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\hskip 1.01749pt\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}\hskip 1.01749pt\rrparenthesis$ should only be performed if ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}$ is a globally unique name; otherwise we break the promise of a write-once memory model. To this effect, we allow name shadowing while an ampar is closed, but as soon as an ampar is open, it should have globally unique hole names. This restriction is enforced in rule {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:Ty-ectxs-OpenAmpar}{{{{Ty-ectxs-OpenAmpar}}}}} by premise ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral hnames\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}~\mathtt{\#\#}~{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral hnames\pdfliteral}(}}\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}$, requiring hole name sets from ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}$ and $\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}}$ to be disjoint when an open ampar focusing component is created during reduction of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$. Likewise, any hollow constructor written to a hole should have globally unique hole names. We assume that hole names are natural numbers for simplicity's sake.
\par To obtain globally fresh names, in the premises of the corresponding rules, we first set{\\
}${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}={\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral max\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\mathsf{\pdfliteral hnames\pdfliteral}(}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\cup}}\,\{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{)}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{+}}\hskip-0.77498pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathtt{1}}$ or similar definitions for ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime\prime}}}$ (see in \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:sem}) to find the next unused name. Then we use either the \emph{shifted set} ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}$ or the \emph{conditional shift operator} ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}$ as defined in \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:sem} to replace all names or just specific one with fresh unused names.
We extend \emph{conditional shift} $\mathord{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}$ to arbitrary values, terms, and typing contexts in the obvious way (keeping in mind that $_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H^{\prime}}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}$ binds the names in ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H^{\prime}}}$).
\par Rules {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:Ampar-Open}{{{{Ampar-Open}}}}} and {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:Ampar-Close}{{{{Ampar-Close}}}}} dictate how and when a closed ampar (a value) is converted to an open ampar (a focusing component) and vice-versa, and they make use of the shifting strategy we've just introduced. With {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:Ampar-Open}{{{{Ampar-Open}}}}}, the hole names bound by the ampar gets renamed to fresh ones, and the left-hand side gets attached to the focusing component $^{\text{op}\!}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.36617pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime}}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~\raisebox{0.69371pt}{$\scriptstyle[]$}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}$ while the right-hand side (containing destinations) is substituted in the body of the ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$ statement (which becomes the new term under focus). The rule {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:Ampar-Close}{{{{Ampar-Close}}}}} triggers when the body of a ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$ statement has reduced to a value. In that case, we can close the ampar, by popping the focusing component from the stack ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}$ and merging back with ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}$ to form a closed ampar again.
\par In rule {\hyperlink{ottalt:rule:ott:FillComp-Red}{{{{FillComp-Red}}}}}, we write the left-hand side ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}$ of a closed ampar $_{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}$ to a hole ${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}$ that is part of a structure with holes somewhere inside ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}$. This results in the composition of two structures with holes. Because we dissociate ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}$ that were previously bound together by the ampar connective (${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}$ is merged with another structure, while ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}$ becomes the new focus), their hole names are no longer bound, so we need to make them globally unique, as we do when an ampar is opened with ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$. This renaming is carried out by the conditional shift ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{H}}\raisebox{-0.52309pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{${\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{=}}}}$}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h^{\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{]}}$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Type Safety} With the semantics now defined, we can state the usual type safety theorems:
\par\begin{theorem}If $\,\boldsymbol{\vdash}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\,\big]\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\,\big]~~\longrightarrow~~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}\,\big]$ then $\,\boldsymbol{\vdash}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}\,\big]\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}$.
\end{theorem}
\par\begin{theorem}If $\,\boldsymbol{\vdash}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\,\big]\boldsymbol{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}$ and $\forall{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}{\scriptstyle\bigl[}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}{\scriptstyle\bigr]}\neq\raisebox{0.70831pt}{$\scriptstyle[]$}{\scriptstyle\bigl[}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}{\scriptstyle\bigr]}$ then $\exists{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}\,\big]~~\longrightarrow~~{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}~\big[\,{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t^{\prime}}}\,\big]$.
\end{theorem}
\par A command of the form $\raisebox{0.69371pt}{$\scriptstyle[]$}{\scriptstyle\bigl[}{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}{\scriptstyle\bigr]}$ cannot be reduced further, as it only contains a fully determined value, and no pending computation. This it is the stopping point of the reduction, and any well-typed command eventually reaches this form.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Formal Proof of Type Safety}
\par
\par We've proved type preservation and progress theorems with the \hrefhttps://coq.inria.fr/. The artifact containing the machine-verified proofs is available at \url{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.14982363}. One can check if a newer version is available using \url{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.14534422} or \url{https://github.com/tweag/destination-calculus}.
\par Turning to a proof assistant was a pragmatic choice: typing context handling in $\lambda_{d}${} can be quite finicky, and it was hard, without computer assistance, to make sure that we hadn't made mistakes in our proofs. The version of $\lambda_{d}${} that we've proved is written in Ott~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{sewell_ott_2007}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, the same Ott file is used as a source for this article, making sure that we've proved the same system as we're presenting; though some visual simplification is applied by a script to produce the version in the article.
\par
\par Most of the proof was done by an author with little prior experience with Coq. This goes to show that Coq is reasonably approachable even for non-trivial development. The proof is about 7000 lines long, and contains nearly 500 lemmas. Many of the cases of the type preservation and progress lemmas are similar. To handle such repetitive cases, the use of a large-language-model based autocompletion system has proven quite effective.
\par The proofs aren't particularly elegant. For instance, we don't have any abstract formalization of semirings: it was more expedient to brute-force the properties we needed by hand. We've observed up to 232 simultaneous goals, but a computer makes short work of this: it was solved by a single call to the \verb|congruence| tactic. Nevertheless there are a few points of interest.
\par First, we represent contexts as finite-domain functions, rather than as syntactic lists. This works much better when defining sums of context. There are a handful of finite-function libraries in the ecosystem, but we needed finite dependent functions (because the type of binders depend on whether we're binding a variable name or a hole name). This didn't exist, but for our limited purpose, it ended up not being too costly rolling our own (about 1000 lines of proofs). The underlying data type is actual functions: this was simpler to develop, but in exchange equality gets more complex than with a bespoke data type.
\par Secondly, Addition of context is partial since we can only add two binding of the same name if they also have the same type. Instead of representing addition as a binary function to an optional context, we represent addition as a total function to contexts, but we change contexts to allow faulty bindings on some names. This works well better for our Ott-written rules, at the cost of needing well-formedness preconditions in the premises of typing rules as well as some lemmas.
\par Finally, to simplify equalities mostly, we assumed a few axioms: functional extensionality, classical logic, and indefinite description:
\par\begin{verbatim}
Axiom constructive_indefinite_description :
forall (A : Type) (P : A->Prop), (exists x, P x) -> { x : A | P x }.
\end{verbatim}
This isn't particularly elegant: we could have avoided some of these axioms at the price of more complex development. But for the sake of this article, we decided to favor expediency over elegance.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Implementation of \destcalculus{} Using in-Place Memory Mutations}
\par The formal language presented in~\lx@cref{creftypeplural~refnum}{sec:syntax-type-system} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{sec:ectxs-sem} is not meant to be implemented as-is.
\par First, $\lambda_{d}${} doesn't have recursion, this would have obscured the presentation of the system. However, adding a standard form of recursion doesn't create any complication.
\par Secondly, ampars are not managed linearly in $\lambda_{d}${}; only destinations are. That is to say that an ampar can be wrapped in an exponential, e.g. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Mod}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}}\,{}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{0}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}$ (representing a difference list $0{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt\phantom{l}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}$ that can be used non-linearly), and then used twice, each time in a different way:
\par\begin{minipage}[172.5pt]\vskip-1.42271pt\begin{center}\begin{minipage}[155.24895pt]{\small${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral case\pdfliteral}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Mod}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}}\,{}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{0}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\boxed{\hskip 1.1625pt{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}\hskip 1.1625pt}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\mathrel{\hskip-0.79999pt\raisebox{-5.03749pt}{\textasciicircum}}\hskip-2.0pt}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.9453,0.293,0.1758}\mathit{h}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\rangle}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral of\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Mod}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}\,\boldsymbol{\mapsto}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.3398,0.2148}\mathit{\backslash\!n}}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.3398,0.2148}\mathit{\langle\ldots\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral let\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral append\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{1}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral in\pdfliteral}}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.3398,0.2148}\mathit{\backslash\!n}}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.3398,0.2148}\mathit{\langle\ldots\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral let\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathrel{\!\raisebox{0.34872pt}{$\mathop{:}$\hskip-0.38751pt}\resizebox{404136}{0}{=}\hskip-0.77498pt}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral append\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{2}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral in\pdfliteral}}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.3398,0.2148}\mathit{\backslash\!n}}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.3398,0.2148}\mathit{\langle\ldots\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.3398,0.2148}\mathit{\langle\ldots\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral toList\pdfliteral}}~({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral concat\pdfliteral}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}})$}\end{minipage}\end{center}\vskip-1.42271pt\end{minipage}$~\longrightarrow^{*}~\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{0}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{0}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{::}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{[]}}${\\[1.9919pt]
}
\par It may seem counter-intuitive at first, but this program is valid and safe in $\lambda_{d}${}. Thanks to the renaming discipline we detailed in~\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{ssec:sem}, every time an ampar is operated over with ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$, its hole names are renamed to fresh ones. One way we can support this is to allocate a fresh copy of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{x}}$ every time we call ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral append\pdfliteral}}$ (which is implemented in terms of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$), in a copy-on-write fashion. This way filling destinations is still implemented as mutation.
\par However, this is a long way from the efficient implementation promised in \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{sec:working-with-dests}. Copy-on-write can be optimized using fully-in-place functional programming ~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{lorenzen_fp_2023}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, where, thanks to reference counting, we don't need to perform a copy when the difference list isn't aliased.
\par An alternative is to refine the linear type system further in order to guarantee that ampars are unique and avoid copy-on-write altogether. We held back from doing that in the formalization of $\lambda_{d}${} as it obfuscates the presentation of the system without adding much in return.
\par To make ampars linear, we follow a recipe proposed by~\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{spiwack_linearly_2022}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} and introduce a new type ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}$, together with primitives ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral dup\pdfliteral}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral drop\pdfliteral}}$. We also switch ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$ for ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes IP\pdfliteral}}}$:\par\vskip-1.42271pt\begin{center}\begin{minipage}[310.4979pt]{\small$\phantom{a}\!\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[t]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral dup\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}\\[1.9919pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral drop\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}\\[1.9919pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes IP\pdfliteral}}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}\end{array}$}\end{minipage}\end{center}\vskip-1.42271pt
\par For the in-place system to work, we consider that a linear root token variable, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}$, is available to a program. ``Closed'' programs can now typecheck in the non-empty context $\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}:\!_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}\}$. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}$ can be used to create new tokens ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{k}}}}}$ via ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral dup\pdfliteral}}$, but each of these tokens still has to be used linearly.
\par Ampar produced by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes IP\pdfliteral}}}$ have a linear dependency on a variable ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{k}}}}}$. If an ampar produced by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes IP\pdfliteral}}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{k}}}}}$ were to be used twice in a block ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}$, then ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}$ would require a typing context $\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{k}}}}}:\!_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}\}$, that itself would require ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{tok_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}$ to have multiplicity ${\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}$ too. Thus the program would be rejected.
\par An alternative to having a linear root token variable is to add a primitive function{\\
}${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral withToken\pdfliteral}}~\boldsymbol{:}~{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}\,_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}\,_{\,}\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\multimap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\omega}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\infty}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}$ that fulfill the same goal.
\par Now that ampars are managed linearly, we can change the allocation and renaming mechanisms:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@the hole name for a new ampar is chosen fresh right from the start (this corresponds to a new heap allocation);
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@adding a new hollow constructor still require freshness for its hole names (this corresponds to a new heap allocation too);
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Using ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral upd\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes\pdfliteral}}}$ over an ampar and filling destinations or composing two ampars using $\triangleleft\raisebox{0.39835pt}{$\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\circ}$}$ no longer require any renaming: we have the guarantee that the all the names involved are globally fresh, and can only be used once, so we can do in-place memory updates.
\end{itemize}
\par$\lambda_{d}${} extended with ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Token}}$s and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral new\pdfliteral}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\pdfliteral\ltimes IP\pdfliteral}}}$ is in fact very close to the implementation described in~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{bagrel_destination-passing_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. Our claim of efficiency is thus based on the results published in the latter and also \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{lorenzen_searchtree_2024,bour_tmc_2021}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, as an hypothetical implementation of $\lambda_{d}${} would mostly resort to the same memory operations -- that is, in-place updates in functional settings.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{From Purely Linked Structures to More Efficient Memory Forms}
\par In $\lambda_{d}${} we only have binary product in sum types. However, it's very straightforward to extend the language and implement destination-based building for n-ary sums of n-ary products, with constructors for each variant having multiple fields directly, instead of each field needing an extra indirection as in the binary sum of products ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\oplus}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{(}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\otimes}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{U}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{)}}$. This is, in fact, already implemented in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{bagrel_destination-passing_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} without any issues. However, it's probably better for field's values to still be represented by pointers.
\par Indeed, composition of incomplete structures relies on the idea that destinations pointing to holes of a structure ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$ will still be valid if ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$ get assigned to a field ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}$ of a bigger structure ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v^{\prime}}}$. That's true indeed if just the address of ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$ is written to ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v^{\prime}}}\!.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}$. However, if ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$ is moved into ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v^{\prime}}}$ completly (i.e. if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{f}}$ is an in-place/unpacked field), then the pointers representing destinations of ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{v}}$ are now invalid.
\par Our early experiments around DPS support for unpacked fields seem to indicate that we would need two classes of destinations, one supporting composition (for indirected fields) and one disallowing it (for unpacked fields).
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Related Work}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Destination-Passing Style for Efficient Memory Management}
\par\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{shaikhha_destination-passing_2017}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} present a destination-based intermediate language for a functional array programming language, with destination-specific optimizations, that boasts near-C performance.
\par This is the most comprehensive evidence to date of the benefits of destination-passing style for performance in functional languages, although their work is on array programming, while this article focuses on linked data structures. They can therefore benefit from optimizations that are perhaps less valuable for us, such as allocating one contiguous memory chunk for several arrays.
\par The main difference between their work and ours is that their language is solely an intermediate language: it would be unsound to program in it manually. We, on the other hand, are proposing a type system to make it sound for the programmer to program directly with destinations.
\par We see these two aspects as complementing each other: good compiler optimizations are important to alleviate the burden from the programmer and allow high-level abstraction; having the possibility to use destinations in code affords the programmer more control, should they need it.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Tail Modulo Constructor}
\par Another example of destinations in a compiler's optimizer is~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{bour_tmc_2021}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. It's meant to address the perennial problem that the map function on linked lists isn't tail-recursive, hence consumes stack space. The observation is that there's a systematic transformation of functions where the only recursive call is under a constructor to a destination-passing tail-recursive implementation.
\par Here again, there's no destination in user land, only in the intermediate representation. However, there is a programmatic interface: the programmer annotates a function like
\begin{verbatim}
let[@tail_mod_cons] rec map =
\end{verbatim}
to ask the compiler to perform the translation. The compiler will then throw an error if it can't. This way, contrary to the optimizations in~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{shaikhha_destination-passing_2017}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, it is entirely predictable.
\par This has been available in OCaml since version 4.14. This is the one example we know of of destinations built in a production-grade compiler. Our $\lambda_{d}${} makes it possible to express the result tail-modulo-constructor in a typed language. It can be used to write programs directly in that style, or it could serve as a typed target language for an automatic transformation. On the flip-side, tail modulo constructor is too weak to handle our difference lists or breadth-first traversal examples.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{A Functional Representation of Data Structures With a Hole}
\par The idea of using linear types as a foundation of a functional calculus in which incomplete data structures can exist and be composed as first class values dates back to~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. Our system is strongly inspired by theirs. In~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, a first-class structure with a hole is called a \emph{hole abstraction}. Hole abstractions are represented by a special kind of linear functions with bespoke restrictions. As with any function, we can't pattern-match on their output (or pass it to another function) until they have been applied; but they also have the restriction that we cannot pattern-match on their argument ---the \emph{hole variable}--- as that one can only be used directly as argument of data constructors, or of other hole abstractions. The type of hole abstractions, ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}})hfun}}$ is thus a weak form of linear function type ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\multimap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}}}$.
\par In~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, it's only ever possible to represent structures with a single hole. But this is a rather superficial restriction. The author doesn't comment on this, but we believe that this restriction only exists for convenience of the exposition: the language is lowered to a language without function abstraction and where composition is performed by combinators. While it's easy to write a combinator for single-argument-function composition, it's cumbersome to write combinators for functions with multiple arguments. But having multiple-hole data structures wouldn't have changed their system in any profound way.
\par The more important difference is that while their system is based on a type of linear functions, ours is based on the linear logic's ``par'' type. In classical linear logic, linear implication ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}\multimap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}}}$ is reinterpreted as ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\mathop{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{\&}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}^{\perp}}}$. We, likewise, reinterpret ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}})hfun}}$ as ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}$ (a sort of weak ``par'').
\par A key consequence is that destinations ---as first-class representations of holes--- appear naturally in $\lambda_{d}${}, while \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} doesn't have them. This means that using~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, or the more recent but similarly expressive system from~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{lorenzen_searchtree_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, one can implement the examples with difference lists and queues from~\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{ssec:efficient-queue}, but couldn't do our breadth-first traversal example from~\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{sec:bft}, since it requires to be able to store destinations in a structure.
\par Nevertheless, we still retain the main restrictions that \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} places on hole abstractions. For instance, we can't pattern-match on ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}$ in (unapplied) ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}})hfun}}$; so in $\lambda_{d}${}, we can't act directly on the left-hand side ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}$ of ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{S}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\ltimes}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}$, only on the right-hand side ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}$. Similarly, hole variables can only be used as arguments of constructors or hole abstractions; it's reflected in $\lambda_{d}${} by the fact that the only way to act on destinations is via fill operations, with either hollow constructors or another ampar.
\par The ability to manipulate destinations, and in particular, store them, does come at a cost though: the system needs this additional notion of ages to ensure that destinations are used soundly. On the other hand, our system is strictly more general, in that \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}'s system can be embedded in $\lambda_{d}${}, and if one stays in this fragment, we're never confronted with ages.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Destination-Passing Style Programming: A Haskell Implementation}
\par The system introduced in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{bagrel_destination-passing_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} is very similar to $\lambda_{d}${}: it has a destination type, and a \emph{par}-like construct (called ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{Incomplete}}$), where only the right-hand side can be modified; together these elements give extra expressiveness to the language compared to~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{minamide_functional_1998}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
\par In that system, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{d}}\blacktriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}$ requires ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}$ to be unrestricted, while in $\lambda_{d}${}, ${\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.3906,0.3906}\mathsf{t}}$ can be linear. The consequence is that in~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{bagrel_destination-passing_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, destinations can be stored in data structures but not in data structures with holes; so in a breadth-first search algorithm like in~\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{sec:bft}, the queue has to be built using normal constructors, and cannot use destination-filling primitives. Therefore both normal constructors and DPS primitives must coexist, while in $\lambda_{d}${}, only DPS primitives are required to bootstrap the system, as we later derive normal constructors from them. In exchange, just linearity is enough to make \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{bagrel_destination-passing_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} system safe.
\par A more profound difference is that this previous work describe a practical implementation of destination passing for an existing functional language, while here we present a more theoretical framework that is meant to justify safety of DPS implementations (such as \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{bagrel_destination-passing_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} itself).
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Semi-Axiomatic Sequent Calculus}
\par In~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{deyoung_sax_2020}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} constructors return to a destination rather than allocating memory. It is very unlike the other systems described in this section in that it's completely founded in the Curry-Howard isomorphism. Specifically it gives an interpretation of a sequent calculus which mixes Gentzen-style deduction rules and Hilbert-style axioms. As a consequence, the \emph{par} connective is completely symmetric, and, unlike our ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\lfloor}}\,\!{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{\rfloor}}$ type, their dualization connective is involutive.
\par The cost of this elegance is that computations may try to pattern-match on a hole, in which case they must wait for the hole to be filled. So the semantics of holes is that of a future or a promise. In turns this requires the semantics of their calculus to be fully concurrent, which is a very different point in the design space.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Rust Lifetimes}
\par Rust uses a system of lifetimes (see e.g. \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{pearce_lifetime_2021}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}) to ensure that borrows don't live longer than what they reference. It plays a similar role as our system of ages.
\par Rust lifetimes are symbolic. Borrows and moves generate constraints (inequalities of the form $\alpha\leqslant\beta$) on the symbolic lifetimes. For instance, that the lifetime of a reference is larger than the lifetime of any structure the reference is stored in. Without such constraints, Rust would have similar problems to those of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{sec:scope-escape-dests}. The borrow checker then checks that the constraints are solvable. This contrasts with $\lambda_{d}${} where ages are set explicitly, with no analysis needed.
\par Another difference between the two systems is that $\lambda_{d}${}'s ages (and modes in general) are relative. An explicit modality ${\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.1875,0.4375}\mathsf{!}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}\hskip-0.40685pt{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}}}$ must be used when a part has an age different than its parent, and means that the part is ${\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}k}$ scope older than the parent. On the other hand, Rust's lifetimes are absolute, the lifetime of a part is tracked independently of the lifetime of its parent.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Oxidizing OCaml}
\par\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{lorenzen_oxidizing_2024}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} present an extension of the OCaml type system to support modes. Their modes are split along three different ``axes'', among which affinity and locality are comparable to our multiplicities and ages.
Like our multiplicities, there are two modes for affinity \verb|once| and \verb|many|, though in~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{lorenzen_oxidizing_2024}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, \verb|once| supports weakening, whereas $\lambda_{d}${}'s ${\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{1}}$ multiplicity is properly linear (proper linearity matters for destination lest we end up reading uninitialized memory).
\par Locality tracks scope. There are two locality modes, \verb|local| (doesn't escape the current scope) and \verb|global| (can escape the current scope). The authors present their locality mode as a drastic simplification of Rust's lifetime system, which nevertheless fits their need.
\par However, such a simplified system would be a bit too weak to track the scope of destinations. The observation is that if destinations from two nested scopes are given the same mode, then we can't safely do anything with them, as it would be enough to reproduce the counterexamples of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{sec:scope-escape-dests}. So in order to type the breadth-first traversal example of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{sec:bft}, where destinations are stored in a structure, we need at least ${\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\nu}}$ (for the current scope), ${\color[rgb]{0.7656,0.1797,0.5313}\mathsf{\uparrow}}$ (for the previous scope exactly), plus at least one extra mode for the rest of the scopes (destinations of this generic age cannot be safely used). It turns out that such systems with finitely many ages are incredibly easy to get wrong, and it was in fact much simpler to design a system with infinitely many exact ages.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion and Future Work}
\par Using a system of ages in addition to linearity, $\lambda_{d}${} is a purely functional calculus which supports destinations in a very flexible way. It subsumes existing calculi from the literature for destination passing, allowing both composition of data structures with holes and storing destinations in data structures. Data structures are allowed to have multiple holes, and destinations can be stored in data structures that, themselves, have holes. The latter is the main reason to introduce ages and is key to $\lambda_{d}${}'s flexibility.
\par We don't anticipate that a system of ages like $\lambda_{d}${} will actually be used in a programming language: it's unlikely that destinations are so central to the design of a programming language that it's worth baking them so deeply in the type system. Perhaps a compiler that makes heavy use of destinations in its optimizer could use $\lambda_{d}${} as a typed intermediate representation. But, more realistically, our expectation is that $\lambda_{d}${} can be used as a theoretical framework to analyze destination-passing systems: if an API can be defined in $\lambda_{d}${} then it's sound.
\par In fact, we plan to use this very strategy to design an API for destination passing in Haskell, leveraging only the existing linear types, but retaining the possibility of storing destinations in data structures with holes.
\par\lx@newpage{}
\par
